# Scene 1 - from Viewpoint Character: Lucien Kael's point of view



They called it a council chamber, but the word was politeness. House Kael’s sanctum had the acoustics of a cathedral and the climate of a tomb. At midnight, even the ghosts kept to their script.



Lucien stood at the threshold for the span of a measured breath. Cold marble underfoot; incense cut with iron filings in the air. Overhead, the vault loomed—gilded ribs webbing a dome painted with scenes of founding, betrayal, slow redemption. Every square inch ordered, every line doubled by reflection in the polished obsidian of the round table below.



Silver candelabras lit the chamber. Not merely light: a surgical clarity, candles snuffed and replaced in silent rotation so no flame ever wore down to a gutter. The arrangement cast faces into relief and left their eyes as voids. Around the table, Dominion elders sat: arrayed by age, power, the luck of surviving. Their robes: black, the thread veined in a shade of red not found in modern dye.



Between each pair of columns, crimson banners hung—a pageant of glyphs so old even Lucien hesitated to translate. In their script, a single error could fracture ten generations. He knew this because the Kael line had made such an error once, and he wore the consequence at his wrist.



He advanced three paces and let the doors boom closed behind. The sound set the room’s rhythm, joined by the unsteady tick of his pocket watch. He wore it under his waistcoat tonight, the case warm against his skin. The watch never left him in a Council session. The elders believed it a Kael affectation. Only Lucien knew what beat behind the cracked face—half a second late, sometimes catching up, sometimes slipping. Tonight it pulsed in sync with the vein at his throat.



High Inquisitor Marcellus Virelli rose with a grace engineered to draw all attention. His scarlet vestments moved as if underwater; every gold accent trapped the candlelight and threw it back as challenge. The stole over his shoulders was white, hemmed in silver, embroidered with a line of text from the first Concord.



Lucien’s eyes traced it. He noted the difference from the copy in his own study—two words replaced, a silent contest in doctrine. He almost smiled.



Virelli’s face was ageless, the kind you find on old coins and old enemies. He placed both hands on the table—fingers long, nails perfect, the left hand carrying a ring so old it predated blood as currency.



He spoke first, the way the Council demanded.



“Curator Kael,” Virelli said. His voice always found the acoustic sweet spot. “You know why we convene tonight.”



“I have suspicions,” Lucien replied, giving the tilt of the head required for mutual respect.



“Let us not tire the air,” Virelli said, gesturing. “The matter before us: the Louisiana witch, Seraphine Vael. Her interference has crossed from local to strategic.”



The name broke the hush. Small, involuntary shifts among the elders—one cleared a throat, one licked a tooth. Virelli let the ripples subside before he continued.



“The Dominion has reached its verdict,” Virelli said. He drew a parchment from a hidden fold and slid it, with two fingers, across the obsidian. The wax was fresh, red as arterial spray, the seal a hybrid of old House sigils and the more recent Mark of Saint Blood.



Lucien did not reach for the writ. “My understanding,” he said, “is that the witch is not in our custody.”



“The writ is for you,” Virelli said. “You alone have established communication with the subject. The Council believes she will respond to you, where she would resist any hand from the Order.”



A legalistic flicker of the eyes: it’s your problem, solve it.



Lucien felt the weight in the next breath. To the left, a Kael cousin stared at him over the bridge of her nose. To the right, a baron from Graz tapped the table three times, a private code of discomfort.



“What is the Council’s will?” Lucien asked. He kept his voice flat, neither defensive nor eager. The tension would do the work.



“Bring the witch into Dominion control,” Virelli said. “Or eliminate her.” He let the words echo. “We prefer compliance. We expect efficiency.”



Lucien tapped his fingers to his watch. The pulse within it went sharp, then reasserted the lag. “You consider her an existential threat?”



“She is conducting resonance experiments without sanction,” Virelli said. “The side effects have crossed three continents. The Kael House alone lost three adjutants to dissonance last month.”



Lucien said nothing. He remembered the Vienna gala—how the color had drained from the room, how the old countess had wept a single golden tear. He wondered if Virelli could still weep.



“You have already breached,” Virelli said. “More than once. Our intelligence places you in direct contact.”



Lucien raised an eyebrow. “Is the Council monitoring my correspondence?”



A ripple of amusement, quickly flattened. “We monitor everything, Lucien,” Virelli said. “You of all people should not be surprised.”



The silence thickened. Lucien reached, this time, for the tuning tool in his breast pocket. He did not use it, but rolled it between thumb and forefinger, letting the weighted ends keep time with the seconds.



Virelli watched this, then let his gaze go to the candelabra. Wax spilled over one side, each drop landing like a punctuation mark.



“Your loyalty is not in question,” Virelli said, “but your sympathy may be. You have always favored the logic of difference over the clarity of unity. It is time to set that aside.”



Lucien looked at the writ. He could see his reflection in the obsidian table, made strange by the convex curve. A line of script on the parchment caught the candle, and for a moment he read only the threat, not the sentence.



He let the moment hold.



“May I speak freely, Your Eminence?” he asked.



Virelli inclined his head. “Please.”



Lucien spoke not to the Inquisitor but to the Council as a body. “The world changes whether we manage it or not. Our kind has survived by adaptation—not by doubling down on laws that have failed, but by learning from the ones who break them. What Seraphine Vael does, she does because the old boundaries are inadequate.”



“She breaks them because she cannot bear their cost,” Virelli said, soft as a razor strop.



Lucien shrugged. “And if the cost is to silence a frequency the world needs?”



The baron from Graz grunted, a sound like stone against stone. The cousin to his left leaned back, calculating angles.



Virelli showed a small, predatory smile. “We have always valued your counsel, Lucien. But this is not an advisory matter. The Council acts.”



He nodded at the parchment. “You have until the equinox. Bring the witch, or bring her end.”



The watch at Lucien’s wrist jumped. He knew the sign: somewhere, she was calling. Or warning. Or both.



He palmed the tuning tool once, then set it on the table, a gesture of respect, a declaration of intent.



“I will do as the Council requires,” Lucien said, “but not as it fears.”



He stood, formal. He reached for the writ, felt the weight of the wax, the cold of the obsidian underneath. He did not break the seal.



He bowed once, from the shoulders.



Virelli’s smile was fixed now, more mask than face. “I trust you will make the right decision, Curator Kael.”



Lucien took the writ and the tuning tool, turned, and walked the length of the chamber. The elders tracked him with their eyes, the way wolves do a limping peer.



The doors parted on silent hinges.



Behind him, the chamber hummed with unspent argument. Ahead, the corridor glowed silver-blue, and the air tasted briefly of hope.



He walked on, the watch ticking irregular, already planning how to rewrite the script the world had handed him.





# Scene 2 - from Viewpoint Character: Lucien Kael's point of view



In the antechamber, Lucien set the writ on the marble plinth and examined it with a surgeon’s attention. The wax was still warm, which meant Virelli had pressed the seal himself; a personal touch meant to telegraph sincerity or, more likely, control. The stamp’s design—Order of Saint Blood, scrolled with the ancient language—made the red of the wax darker, more accusation than formality.



He did not break it.



Instead he watched his reflection in the polished surface. Silver flicked through his eyes, a color that once would have condemned him as a hybrid. Now it passed for pedigree, if you had the right enemies and the wrong friends. In the candlelight, his pupils narrowed until they looked rimmed with metal.



He reset his cuffs, smoothed the lapel of the charcoal jacket, felt the old bone-deep cold ignite at the base of his throat. The Council had choreographed the night so he would have no time to think between orders and obedience. The next act, of course, was staged for maximum leverage.



Virelli entered as if summoned, bringing a wind of authority behind him. His vestments seemed to deepen the shadow, every step rebuking Lucien for standing anywhere but in a grave.



Virelli did not bother with small talk. He motioned to a circle of high-backed chairs set around a table so polished it mirrored everything above and nothing below. Lucien took the seat at the far side, directly opposite the empty chair marked by the old Kael crest.



“Do you understand the weight of what you carry?” Virelli asked. His tone was catechism, not curiosity.



Lucien glanced at the writ. “I understand it is heavier than it appears.”



Virelli’s mouth bent in what passed for a smile. “It is more than a verdict. It is an instruction, and a test.”



Lucien waited, hands in lap, one thumb circling his watch under the table. The rhythm in his veins was a drumroll, but he let the seconds drag.



Virelli placed his hands—white-gloved, each finger capped with a signet—atop the table. “Seraphine Vael’s transgressions are not merely local. She has fractured the Veil in a way that cannot be mended by force or censure. The resonance she manipulates is an existential threat.”



Lucien let his eyes go blank, as if absorbing the information for the first time. “You believe her capable of destroying the Dominion.”



“Not by choice,” Virelli said, voice darkening, “but by entropy. Her actions unravel centuries of careful balance. Every step she takes tears at the boundary between order and chaos.”



Lucien toyed with the idea of laughter. “Or perhaps it simply exposes that the boundary was never so stable to begin with.”



A flicker of warning crossed Virelli’s face; not anger, but the calculus of risk. “You are clever, Lucien. But cleverness does not excuse sentimentality. You cannot protect the witch. Not from us, not from herself.”



Lucien studied the table’s reflection. The empty chair beside him was a pointed absence, a seat held open for a ghost or a memory. He wondered if Seraphine would have enjoyed the view. The thought slipped a smile past his guard.



Virelli’s patience was engineered to last centuries, but Lucien could see the fault lines. “You take this lightly?”



“I take it personally,” Lucien said, matching the Inquisitor’s cadence. “Which is how I know your argument is incomplete.”



Virelli inclined his head. “Complete it for me.”



Lucien leaned forward, his own hands now on the table, fingers splayed as if to catch the next word before it landed. “You say the witch’s experiments border on heresy. I say they approach something necessary. The world is failing by degrees—the humans see it in their weather, we see it in our colors. What you call threat, I call warning. What if she is only the first to notice?”



Virelli’s eyes darkened. “Your House once believed itself the guardian of truth. Now you risk it all for a witch and a hypothesis.”



“I risk it for the possibility of a future,” Lucien said. “One where the Council does not dictate which frequencies survive.”



A stilled silence. Even the candelabra flames seemed to hesitate.



Virelli’s next words came measured, edged with centuries of precedent. “Your refusal would be classified as sedition. Perhaps more. The Council has always been lenient with the Kael bloodline, but you push the boundary.”



Lucien’s pulse quickened, but he let the moment hold. “And if I accept the charge?”



“You would surrender the witch to Dominion authority. She would be tried in accordance with the Order. No pain, only necessary silence.”



Lucien pictured Seraphine in the council’s grasp—her defiance, the way she never let a silence go unfilled. He pictured Virelli, the satisfaction of compliance. He pictured the empty chair.



He reached for the writ, thumbed the edges, feeling the fibers catch on his skin.



“And if I refuse?” he asked, soft as dust.



Virelli’s jaw moved, the barest tic betraying effort. “Then you join her. You are not the first Curator to mistake self-interest for loyalty.”



Lucien set the writ back on the plinth. “I was loyal before you were born.”



Virelli blinked. “Then act like it.”



Lucien stood, but left the writ untouched. “The Dominion can have my answer at dawn.”



Virelli did not rise. “I will hold you to it, Curator Kael.”



Lucien nodded, turned, and left the council’s orbit with the sense that he had just stepped off a roof and was waiting for the wind to decide which way to throw him.



Outside the antechamber, the corridor ran blue and black, every footstep echoed to infinity. He held the tuning tool in his palm, squeezed it, let the metal chill his nerves.



He walked until the resonance of the council faded, replaced by his own—hot, insistent, impossible to subdue.



He would not bring Seraphine Vael to heel. He would bring her warning, and let the Dominion chase the future if it dared.



He stepped into the Vienna night and for the first time in decades felt the air around him vibrate with real, living possibility.





# Scene 3 - from Viewpoint Character: Lucien Kael's point of view



He found the council chamber restored to perfect symmetry, as if the night’s argument had never passed through its air. The candelabras stood unwavering, pools of wax erased, every chair reset to its original posture. The only sign of fracture: the writ, centered on the table, red wax glistening as if it had just been poured.



Lucien entered with deliberate slowness, making the room adjust to him rather than the other way around. Every councilor watched. Even the walls, designed to catch whispers and return them as thunder, seemed to hush.



He sat, and for a time, nothing moved.



Then he reached not for the writ, but for the tuning tool in his pocket. He let it rest on the glass-black surface, its ends polished to a mirror finish. With his thumb, he spun it once, the hum so fine it registered only to those with ears trained for dissonance.



Virelli’s hands tensed. “You have considered the Council’s verdict?”



“I have,” Lucien said. “I found it wanting.”



A ripple—impatience, disbelief—crossed the circle. Lucien saw the younger elders tilt forward, old ones grip their armrests. Virelli’s jaw set hard.



Lucien turned the tool again, feeling the tremor ride up his wrist and into his teeth. “You mistake control for compassion, Marcellus. Always have.”



The room inhaled as one.



He removed the Kael signet from his finger and placed it, with a click, atop the writ. “House Kael rejects your edict.”



A century of protocol shattered in a single gesture. The Baroness of Graz gasped. Two councilors recoiled as if the ring were live.



Lucien pressed on, his voice low but edged. “I will not surrender the witch to your inquisition. Nor will I abet the burial of what remains of our future.”



Virelli’s facade finally cracked. He rose, the papal red of his vestments burning in the candlelight. “You risk everything. Exile. Excommunication. For a human witch who treats your House as a curiosity?”



Lucien matched his stance, standing as the chamber echoed the argument back in layers. The pulse behind his eyes flared—the world’s noise, the resonance, and the private current Seraphine had seeded in him.



“For empathy,” he said. “A concept your Order once defended, before it was traded for obedience.”



The baroness whispered something that sounded like sacrilege.



Virelli leaned in, voice a whip. “This is the path that ended the last Concord.”



Lucien smiled, sad and sharp. “Then let it begin a new one.”



The air shifted—dangerous, alive. Lucien could see the idea racing through the council, weighing the cost of the first true schism since the Veil was raised. He could see the fear, but also the hunger.



Virelli’s composure regrew itself, but slower now. He drew himself to full height. “You have chosen your side, old friend. I will remember.”



“Good,” Lucien said, eyes gone silver-bright. “So will I.”



The silence after that had a density, a flavor. Lucien let it ferment, then turned, leaving the signet and the tuning tool behind. As he walked, he could feel the council’s gaze burn between his shoulder blades, their breath tracking his every move.



Outside, the corridor was as cold as the first night he arrived in Vienna. The city’s bells tolled the top of the hour, and from somewhere deep in the earth, a hum answered in perfect, stubborn counterpoint.



He walked the length of the House, out past the waiting Kael guards, who did not bow this time but stood aside, wary and maybe, just maybe, hopeful.



At the street, the aurora cut the sky in a fresh wound of gold and violet. He watched it swirl, let it mark the start of what was next.



He reached for his pocket watch, felt it stutter in his hand, then tick forward—one, two, three—refusing to keep to any clock but his own.



Lucien Kael walked into the night, no longer a Curator, no longer a tool of any Dominion, but something new. He wondered if Seraphine would see him coming.



He hoped she would.

# **Chapter 16**

## **Scene 1 — Lucien Kael**

They called it a council chamber. Politeness. House Kael’s sanctum had the acoustics of a cathedral and the temperature of a tomb. At midnight even the ghosts kept to their script.

Lucien paused on the threshold for a measured breath. Cold marble underfoot. Incense cut with iron filings. Overhead, a gilt-ribbed dome—founding, betrayal, slow redemption painted in orderly frames—reflected in the obsidian round table below.

Candelabras threw surgical light. Flames rotated in silent sequence so no wick burned down to a gutter. Faces were carved from shadow; eyes were voids. Around the table sat Dominion elders: age, power, and luck in black robes veined with a red no modern dye could name.

Between columns, crimson banners fell—a grammar of glyphs old enough to break bloodlines if misread. The Kael line had misread once. Lucien wore the consequence still, invisible as a scar.

He advanced three paces. The doors boomed shut, setting the room’s rhythm. His pocket watch answered with its off-beat tick beneath his waistcoat—half a second late, sometimes catching up. Tonight it pulsed at his throat.

High Inquisitor Marcellus Virelli rose. Scarlet vestments moved as if through water; gold caught light and hurled it back. The stole on his shoulders carried a line from the first Concord—two words altered from the copy in Lucien’s study. Doctrine always edits itself.

“Curator Kael,” Virelli said, voice placed perfectly in the chamber’s sweet spot. “You know why we convene.”

“I have suspicions,” Lucien allowed.

“Let us not tire the air.” Virelli gestured. “The Louisiana witch. Seraphine Vael. Her interference has crossed from local to strategic.”

The name stirred the circle—throats cleared, a lip wet with a tongue. Virelli let the ripples die.

“The Dominion has reached its verdict.” He slid a parchment across the obsidian. Fresh red wax. A seal spliced from old House marks and the Order of Saint Blood.

Lucien did not touch it. “My understanding is that the witch is not in our custody.”

“The writ is for you.” Virelli’s gaze held. “You alone established communication. She may answer you where she would resist the Order.”

A legal flicker: your problem to solve.

Lucien felt the weight of eyes. A Kael cousin watched him over the bridge of her nose. A baron tapped three times on the stone, discomfort in code.

“What is the Council’s will?”

“Bring the witch into Dominion control,” Virelli said. “Or eliminate her.” The echo did the rest. “We prefer compliance. We expect efficiency.”

Lucien tapped two fingers to the watch. The pulse inside sharpened, then resumed its lag. “An existential threat.”

“She conducts resonance experiments without sanction. Side effects across three continents. House Kael alone lost three adjutants to dissonance last month.”

Lucien remembered the Vienna gala, color draining from the room, the old countess weeping a single golden tear. He wondered if Virelli could still weep.

“You have already breached,” Virelli said. “More than once. Our intelligence places you in direct contact.”

“Is the Council reading my correspondence?”

“We monitor everything,” Virelli said, almost kindly. “You, above all, should not be surprised.”

Lucien let the silence thicken until the room felt heavier by a breath. He turned the tuning tool in his pocket with his thumb, the weighted ends keeping time.

“Your loyalty is not in question,” Virelli said. “Your sympathy may be. You favor the logic of difference over the clarity of unity. It is time to set that aside.”

Lucien studied the writ’s reflection in the curved obsidian. For a heartbeat the parchment’s line resolved into threat rather than sentence. He let the moment hold.

“May I speak freely, Your Eminence?”

“Please.”

He addressed the room, not the man. “The world is changing, with or without our management. We’ve survived by adaptation, not by doubling down on laws that no longer hold. What Seraphine Vael does, she does because the old boundaries fail to carry the cost.”

“She breaks them because she cannot bear the cost,” Virelli said, soft as a strop.

“And if silencing her erases a frequency the world needs?”

Stone on stone from Graz; calculation to the left.

Virelli’s smile showed teeth, not joy. “We value your counsel, Lucien. This is not advisory. The Council acts.”

He touched the writ. “Until the equinox. Bring the witch, or bring her end.”

The watch at Lucien’s wrist jumped. Somewhere, she moved. Or warned. Or both.

He set the tuning tool on the table—respect and declaration in one gesture. “I will do as the Council requires,” Lucien said, “but not as it fears.”

He took the writ. He did not break the seal.

He bowed from the shoulders.

“I trust you will make the right decision, Curator Kael,” Virelli said.

Lucien turned and walked the long room under the gaze of wolves who smell a limp.

The doors opened on quiet hinges. Behind him, the chamber hummed with unspent argument. Ahead, the corridor breathed silver-blue and tasted, briefly, of hope.

He walked on, the watch ticking irregular, already rewriting the script he’d been handed.

---

## **Scene 2 — Lucien Kael**

In the antechamber he set the writ on a marble plinth. The wax was still warm. Virelli had pressed the seal himself; control disguised as sincerity. The stamp’s language made the red read darker—accusation masquerading as formality.

He did not break it.

His reflection stared back from the polished stone—silver in the eyes that once would have branded him hybrid. These days pedigree was a matter of enemies and friends. In this light, his pupils rimmed to metal.

He reset his cuffs. Smoothed the lapel. The old bone-cold lit at the base of his throat. The Council had choreographed the night to leave no gap between order and obedience.

Virelli entered with the gravity of weather. Shadows deepened around the vestments; each step suggested a grave was the only proper place for disagreement.

He motioned to a ring of high-backed chairs around a table so polished it reflected everything above and nothing below. Lucien took the seat opposite the empty chair marked with the Kael crest.

“Do you understand the weight of what you carry?” Catechism, not curiosity.

“It is heavier than it appears.”

“It is an instruction and a test.”

Lucien let the seconds drag. One thumb traced the watch under the table; the rhythm in his veins drummed back.

“Seraphine Vael’s transgressions are not local,” Virelli said. “She has fractured the Veil in a way that force and censure cannot mend. The resonance she manipulates is an existential threat.”

“You believe she will destroy the Dominion,” Lucien said, as if tasting the words for the first time.

“Not by intent. By entropy. Every act unravels our balance. Each step tears at the seam between order and ruin.”

“Or exposes that the seam was never as sound as we claimed.”

A flicker of risk in Virelli’s gaze. “Cleverness is not a defense. You cannot protect the witch—from us or from herself.”

Lucien studied the table’s mirror. The empty chair beside him felt pointed, a seat held for a ghost. He wondered if Seraphine would have enjoyed the view. The thought slipped a smile past his guard.

“You take this lightly?”

“I take it personally,” Lucien said, matching cadence. “Which is how I know your argument is incomplete.”

“Complete it.”

“You call her heretic; I call her necessary. Humans see the failure in their weather. We see it in our colors. What you label threat might be warning. Perhaps she is only the first to say it aloud.”

“Your House once styled itself guardian of truth. Now you risk all for a witch and a hypothesis.”

“I risk it for the possibility of a future,” he said. “One where the Council doesn’t decide which frequencies get to live.”

Silence stilled even the candles.

“Refusal is sedition,” Virelli said at last. “The Council has indulged the Kael line. You press the boundary.”

“And if I accept the charge?”

“You surrender the witch. She is tried by the Order. No pain—only necessary silence.”

Lucien pictured Seraphine in Dominion chains. Pictured the Inquisitor’s relief. Pictured the empty chair.

He thumbed the writ’s edge; the fibers caught his skin.

“And if I refuse?”

“Then you join her.”

“I was loyal before you were born,” Lucien said.

“Then act like it.”

“I will answer at dawn.”

Virelli did not rise. “I will hold you to it.”

Lucien left with the feeling of stepping off a roof and waiting for the wind to choose a side.

The corridor ran long and blue-black. His footsteps doubled into infinity. He squeezed the tuning tool until the metal chilled his nerves.

When the Council’s resonance thinned, his own returned—hot, insistent, unwilling to be subdued.

He would not bring Seraphine to heel. He would bring warning. Let the Dominion chase the future if it dared.

The Vienna night met him alive. For the first time in decades, the air vibrated with possibility.

---

## **Scene 3 — Lucien Kael**

By protocol, the chamber had restored itself: candelabras unwavering, wax erased, chairs returned to posture. Only the writ remained as fracture—centered, red seal bright as a wound.

Lucien entered slowly and made the room adjust to him. Councilors watched. The walls, tuned to catch whispers and return them as thunder, listened.

He sat. Let nothing move.

Then he set the tuning tool on the glass-black table and spun it once. The hum was fine enough that only trained ears caught it. Virelli did.

“You have considered the verdict?”

“I have,” Lucien said. “It is wanting.”

A ripple crossed the circle. Young elders leaned in. Old hands tightened on rests. Virelli’s jaw set.

“You mistake control for compassion,” Lucien said. “You always have.”

He slid the Kael signet from his finger and placed it atop the writ. The click sounded like a century breaking.

“House Kael rejects your edict.”

Protocol shattered. The Baroness of Graz hissed a prayer. Two elders recoiled from the ring’s shadow.

Lucien kept his voice low, edged. “I will not surrender the witch to your inquisition. I will not bury what remains of our future.”

Virelli rose, vestments burning in the light. “You risk exile. Excommunication. For a human who treats your House as a curiosity?”

“For empathy,” Lucien said. “Your Order once defended it before trading it for obedience.”

The baroness whispered something that tasted like blasphemy.

“This is the road that ended the last Concord,” Virelli warned.

“Then let it begin a new one.”

The air changed—dangerous, alive. Lucien watched the idea run the circle, tallying cost and hunger. Fear showed; so did appetite.

Virelli’s composure returned, slower. “You have chosen your side, old friend. I will remember.”

“Good,” Lucien said, eyes gone silver-bright. “So will I.”

Silence took on weight. Lucien let it ferment, then turned. He left the signet and the tuning tool where they lay. Heat burned between his shoulders as the council tracked him to the door.

Outside, the corridor was as cold as his first Vienna night. Bells tolled the hour; somewhere deep in the earth, a hum answered in stubborn counterpoint.

He passed Kael guards who did not bow. They stood aside—wary and, perhaps, a little hopeful.

In the street, the aurora scored the sky with gold and violet. He let it mark the start of what came next.

He drew the pocket watch. It stuttered, then ticked forward—one, two, three—refusing any clock but its own.

Lucien Kael walked into the night—no longer Curator, no longer Dominion’s tool—something new forming in the space rebellion makes. He wondered if Seraphine would see him coming.

He hoped she would.
