repeat for Ch 17: 

# Scene 1



Baz’s boots hit the Paris curb like the world owed him an apology. The air was glassy with cold and the tension of a city clamped in the grip of two centuries’ worth of suppressed magic. In the half-second before the others caught up, he flicked his lighter and ran a thumb down the torch handle, breath fogging the brass. It flared—not orange, not natural, but a bruised violet that cut the darkness and left afterimages on his vision.



Seraphine and Lucien hit the steps a beat behind, breathing hard, but not from the run. That hum was still with them: the electric hunger in every surface, the pulse that kept time in their bones. Caro, barely visible even in the weird torchlight, prowled ahead with a gait that hovered between alley cat and ballet mistress—shoulders sharp, every step calculated to betray nothing and see everything.



Baz gave them a mock salute and started down the spiral. “Ladies, gentleman, and assorted felines—welcome to the ossuaries.” He kept his voice at a hush but couldn’t help the smirk. “Three lefts, two rights, then down the priest’s stairwell. Watch your step. These bones don’t like to be disturbed.”



The entry reeked of cheap limestone and distant sewage. Baz led, torch held high. The walls closed in with the architectural optimism of a grave. Along the way, skulls stared in infinite recursion, some arranged in neat rows, others in flower patterns that mocked the idea of mourning. Every few meters, a section of the wall bulged with femurs or half-shattered pelvises, the pattern repeating with the obsessiveness of the damned.



Lucien kept close to Baz, face tight, lips pale, eyes flickering between the torch and Seraphine. He looked more alive than the last time Baz had seen him, which meant he looked like death in a new suit. “You’ve been down here often?” he murmured.



Baz grinned. “You never forget your first revolution. Or your third. All the real ones end in basements or tunnels. Paris is nothing but a city built on old mistakes.”



Caro dropped to all fours, her tail flickering in and out of reality as she took point. At each junction, she stopped, ears canted back, then bared her teeth and motioned them forward. “We’re alone for now,” she whispered, “but I smell more than one flavor of trouble up the line.” Her eyes flashed gold, then blue. “Someone’s running a drone, but the signature is shit—probably a bat.”



Seraphine laughed under her breath, low and mean. “Dominion never could get subtlety right.”



They hit the first left. The ceiling dropped, the air grew wet, and the bones got older, or at least more honest about it. Baz led them along a rail—ancient, iron, rusted through, but solid enough. Once, this had been for mining. Now it was the only thing that kept you from getting lost forever. He kept one gloved hand on the rail, the other on the torch, and never looked back unless he had to.



“Down here, the walls carry sound,” Baz said. “We’ll hear them before we see them.” He checked his pulse: dead, as usual. It was always the metaphors that stung.



Seraphine stepped careful. Her boots squelched in water, and each time the torch’s reflection caught her face, she looked more like a person Baz could have known in another life—ferocious, tired, stubborn as rot. He liked her for it. “If they’re after us, how long do we have?” she asked.



Lucien answered, not looking at her. “Depends on whether they sent the Inquisition or just a culling party.”



Caro, now full human, doubled back with a shudder. “Both,” she said, rubbing at her forearm. “And someone’s using a resonance tracker. We’re bleeding a signal even a politician could follow.”



Baz pressed the pace. At the second right, he stopped just short of a pillar of skulls arranged in a parody of a wedding cake. He passed the torch in a semicircle to highlight the eyesockets. “See these?” he said, voice low. “Each one remembers a war. If the Dominion wants to dance, let them come.”



Lucien nodded once, the gesture halfway between camaraderie and apology. “We appreciate your guidance, Baz.”



Baz snorted. “You don’t have to sound so damn formal. We’re all rats down here.”



Ahead, a corridor yawned open: wider, more finished, a central path leading to a great arch painted with Latin graffiti and the memory of candle wax. Baz stepped through and the rest followed. The torch cast weird, overlapping shadows—first Lucien’s, then Seraphine’s, then Caro’s slinking at the edge like a curse that wouldn’t leave.



Baz killed the momentum and turned on a heel. “Stop,” he said, all performance gone from his voice. “Listen.”



They did. At first: nothing but the rattle of water and the creak of old stone settling. Then, unmistakable: a three-count of boots, steady, then a pause, then the three-count again, as if someone had scripted the menace.



Caro’s hackles rose; she shimmered to a more catlike state, nails glinting. “Virelli’s men,” she hissed. “They’re faster than I thought.”



Baz felt something tighten in his ribs. Not fear, not quite, but the edge that comes before a break. He motioned everyone forward, fast. “Left, left, then down. Don’t stop for anything.”



They ran.



The tunnels narrowed. Baz banged his elbow on a jutting rib but kept the torch upright. At the next bend, Caro ducked under a collapsed arch and held it for the others. The stone scraped Lucien’s head, drew a thin line of blood that glittered silver before it beaded up and vanished.



Seraphine exhaled hard. “You all right?”



Lucien barely nodded, hand at the wound. “Only pride,” he said.



The corridor descended, spiraling. The walls here were slick with condensation and, now, streaked with lines of violet-gold—at first faint, then thickening. Baz held up the torch, let the fire find the cracks. The color ran in threads down the stone, pulsing with the same rhythm as the world above.



Seraphine pressed a palm to the wall. “Resonance,” she said, voice flat. “It’s everywhere.”



Lucien’s jaw clenched. “The breach is accelerating. If we don’t reach the core—”



“Not now,” Baz cut in. “Survive first. Existential crises later.”



Caro growled, then laughed. “He’s not wrong.”



Baz gave them another left, then a switchback, then they tumbled onto a wide landing, steps spiraling down into darkness. The air changed: warmer, thick with old incense and rot. Baz let the torchlight sweep the stairwell. At the bottom, a gate loomed, black wrought iron and older than any of them.



“Priest’s stairwell,” Baz said. “After this, we’re on holy ground. Even the Dominion gets the shakes.”



He grabbed the gate, shook it once for show, then worked the lock with a small bar he’d hidden in his boot. The trick still worked. The gate creaked open with a sound that echoed forever.



Seraphine led the way down, boots whispering on stone. Lucien followed, then Baz, then Caro last, pausing at the top to check the tunnel behind.



Baz took the torch and watched it flicker against the walls. “We’re not alone anymore,” he said.



Seraphine smiled, all teeth. “We never were.”



As they plunged into the stairs, the resonance bled through every surface, turning each step into a test of nerve. The further down, the brighter the violet-gold lines became, until the torch was outmatched and Baz lowered it, letting the magic light the way.



Above, the footfalls thickened, but the party moved too fast to care. Every so often, Baz caught Caro’s eyes, slits wide, and the two of them shared the kind of understanding only survivors got: the moment wasn’t made for heroes, just the desperate.



At the bottom, they hit a corridor so narrow even Lucien had to turn sideways. The air stank of ozone and memory. The walls thrummed in time with the pulse that never left Baz’s ears.



“Where now?” Seraphine asked.



Baz checked the map behind his eyes and aimed the torch at the seam in the wall. “We follow the color,” he said, and it sounded almost romantic.



They did. The light danced ahead, leading them on. The tunnel curved, then forked, then doubled back, and Baz kept the pace, never hesitating.



Finally, the resonance built to a pitch so high even Caro flinched. Lucien gripped Seraphine’s arm, steadying her. She didn’t shake him off.



The group paused at a junction, catching their breath as the world vibrated around them. Behind, the boots were closer, the voices distinct—Virelli’s men, chanting in Latin, prepping for a kill.



Baz set the torch on a ledge and turned to face the others. “This is the point of no return,” he said, not even trying for a joke. “We can bail left, get lost in the tunnels forever, or go right and hit the breach straight on.”



Caro flexed her claws. “If we go left, we die slower. If we go right—”



“We make history,” Baz finished.



Lucien looked at Seraphine. Her eyes burned gold and violet in the torchlight. “I trust you,” he said.



She grinned, sudden and savage. “Good. Follow me.”



They went right. The tunnel narrowed, then widened, then exploded into an ossuary so vast Baz couldn’t see the end. Stacks of skulls rose to the ceiling, all watching, all remembering.



Behind them, the footfalls hit the landing. Baz grabbed the torch, rolled his shoulders, and signaled the others on. “Three lefts, two rights, priest’s stairwell, then history,” he muttered. “Let’s see if the world’s ready.”



They ran. The light behind them turned red. The resonance ahead turned violet-gold, so bright it painted shadows on their backs. Baz felt the old panic, but this time, he didn’t flinch.



He ran harder, knowing that if he looked back, he’d see Virelli’s men right behind them—hungry, desperate, and wrong.



The torch flickered out as they hit the next turn, but the magic didn’t need it anymore. The walls glowed with a light that came from memory, or hope, or just the refusal to die quietly.



Baz barreled ahead, every breath a dare. Seraphine was just ahead, Lucien beside him, Caro a flicker at his heels.



They plunged deeper, chasing the only thing that ever mattered: the chance to change the story before it ended.



And behind them, the world finally started to catch up.





# Scene 2



The next leg was a fever dream of panic and water.



They pounded down a spiral and hit a corridor half-choked with runoff from the last week’s rain, the kind that worked its way through Paris stone like nothing could stop it. The first step in, ice-cold water ate Baz’s shin to the calf. The second step, it was up to his knee, and Lucien’s hissed curse behind him was all the warning the group got before the rest of them were thigh-deep and wading through what Baz decided to call a sewer only in the most optimistic sense.



The torch sputtered with the wet. Baz shook it, then cupped it in his hand, willing it to live. The violet core guttered, then surged, igniting the old resin on the walls with a flare that made every drop of water glint like mercury. For a second, Baz caught their reflections in the skin of the flood: Seraphine’s jaw locked, her eyes set; Lucien close behind, face smoothed by sheer focus; Caro, water to her waist, snarling at the cold.



“Well, that’s new and terrifying,” Baz said, keeping his voice breezy despite the sharp edge in his chest. “If I start glowing, someone put me out.”



Behind them, the echo of boots went liquid, a slap-slap-slap that grew only faster as they slogged forward. Caro, fully human and sodden, still moved with the ease of something that’d never cared about human joints. She cut through the water like a shark, finding every high spot and pulling the others toward her.



Ahead, the tunnel dipped, the water deepening. Baz kept the torch above his head, but even then the flame barely stayed dry. The air was pure chill; his breath came ragged.



Lucien spoke quietly: “They’re closing.”



“I know,” Baz grunted. He risked a look back. The glow of their pursuers’ lamps splashed and vanished on the bends, but the sound—they’d have minutes at most.



Caro paused at a cross-tunnel, sniffed, then pointed with her chin. “That way. The ossuary vault.”



Seraphine almost slipped on the next step, boot catching on slick limestone. She went down hard, water up to her chest, arms flailing for a hold. Baz reacted without thinking—hooked a fist in her collar and yanked her up with force that surprised even him.



She blinked, coughed, wiped her mouth. “You always this charming?” she rasped.



Baz grinned, all sharp teeth. “You haven’t seen charming yet.” He steadied her, then let go, flexing his hand where he’d grabbed fabric and bone.



They kept going. The water shallowed, then disappeared as the tunnel canted up and left. Baz counted breaths—twenty, then forty, then lost count as adrenaline fried the math. The torch flared again, the color brighter than ever. Baz shook it, earning a cloud of blue smoke and the faint smell of burnt sugar.



Lucien, right at Baz’s shoulder now, muttered, “We’re close. The wall hums with it.”



It did. Every few meters, the limestone vibrated under Baz’s free hand, like a plucked wire running up his arm and straight into his jaw. He started to feel it in his teeth—a low, insistent buzz that only got worse the farther they ran.



Then Caro skidded to a halt, arms out to block the rest. “Wait,” she whispered.



Baz almost ran her down. “What—”



She held up a finger, eyes huge and black. “Trap,” she hissed. “Not ours.”



A second later, the torch picked out the next junction: a corridor wider than any before, lined on both sides with stacks of skulls three meters high. In the center, a patch of dry stone, almost inviting.



Baz slowed, torch low. The boots behind them had gone silent, but he could hear breathing. More than just the four of them.



Caro made a fast hand sign, the kind Baz had only seen in street fights and old revolts—“Hide.” She bolted left, dragging Lucien with her. Baz grabbed Seraphine by the elbow, then pushed them behind the nearest pillar of bones. The torch went out; the only light was the sickly violet bleeding through the seams in the stone.



They crouched. Caro pressed Lucien flat against the wall, barely leaving space for herself. Baz and Seraphine huddled tight, his hand over her mouth before she could object. She glared, but stayed silent.



The footsteps returned—softer now, stealthy. Baz strained his ears. Two, maybe three people, moving with military discipline.



Then a voice: “They’re here.” Baz recognized it—a Dominion dog, standard issue.



Another voice, lower, more dangerous: “Fan out. The cat’s familiar—she’ll double back.”



Baz felt Seraphine tense, her pulse jumping under his palm.



The hunters swept the corridor, light bouncing off the skulls in hard, jagged flashes. Baz watched through the gaps, measuring their progress. The first man passed, lamp high, head on a swivel. The second trailed, slower, gun out, moving like he’d done this in wars that hadn’t made the papers.



The third moved with purpose, more animal than the others—Virelli’s personal flavor, probably. He stopped just meters from where Caro and Lucien hid, then knelt, touching the stones.



Baz held his breath. Seraphine did, too.



The man sniffed the air, then spoke in a language Baz couldn’t place, words sharp and hot. For a second, Baz thought they’d be found, but then Caro’s voice, from farther down the tunnel, rang out—a perfect, echoing meow, bold as a gunshot.



The man and his shadows turned, then bolted after the sound.



Baz released Seraphine, who gave him a look halfway between gratitude and homicide. “Next time,” she said, “I bite.”



Baz winked. “Promises, promises.”



They waited until the voices faded. Only then did Caro and Lucien reappear, both dripping, both grinning like they’d stolen something.



“Nice play,” Baz said.



Caro flicked water from her fingers. “Dominion men. Brains like cinder blocks. Show them a shiny, they chase.”



Lucien straightened his cuffs, gave Baz a nod. “We keep moving. The breach isn’t far.”



Seraphine rolled her shoulders. “Which way?”



Baz re-lit the torch. It sputtered, then stabilized. “This way,” he said, and this time the words carried a new kind of certainty—like the tunnel wasn’t just a tomb but a road, and every wrong turn could still lead somewhere better.



They slipped into the ossuary vault, the skulls watching, and for the first time Baz felt almost at home.



Behind, the water stilled, the hunters gone, at least for now.



Ahead, the walls began to breathe with the color of another world.



Baz led the way, the others right behind, and if his hand trembled on the torch, it was only because there was so much left to burn.





# Scene 3



The tunnel let them go at last, but not before one final indignity.



They spilled into a cul-de-sac of bone and mortar, torchlight dying against the stones, all four of them wheezing and streaked with mud and old history. Baz was the first to recover—he always was—and he pressed a hand against the wall, feeling for the trick his grandfather had sworn by.



Caro stood very still, eyes closed, catching every sound. In that hush, the world could have ended and Baz wouldn’t have noticed—he was so close to the wall, lips moving, counting out the rhythm. When he found it, he glanced at Caro, and she gave a single, sharp nod.



No words passed between them. None needed.



He set his shoulder against the panel and shoved. Stone grated against stone, and the air coughed dust into his mouth. The wall gave, just enough. A slice of black appeared at the seam—no more than a coffin’s width.



Baz stepped back, wiped the sweat from his brow, and looked at the others. “Revolution leaves its marks everywhere,” he said, not quite able to keep the pride out of his voice, even if the effect was ruined by how much he ached.



Lucien arched an eyebrow, approving. “I’d heard the Parisian cells had their ways.”



Baz grinned, teeth bright even in the violet gloom. “We prefer to call them detours.”



He ducked in, torch leading, the others crowding after. The passage was tight—less than a meter wide, lower still—and within steps, they had to hunch, then crawl. The walls pressed in; the smell was antique and sharp with rodent ammonia. Above, centuries of city and bone creaked and shuddered.



Baz led on hands and knees, torch in his teeth, refusing to look back. Seraphine followed, then Lucien, then Caro, who barely fit even in her thin shape.



It was the sound that changed first—a tremor in the dust, a shudder that ran up Baz’s hands and into the marrow. Then came the rumble: soft, ominous, like a cough from the world’s old lungs. Baz braced, shouted back: “Move! We’re about to—”



The tunnel sagged in the middle, a chunk of ceiling dropping to block the path behind Lucien. For a beat, they were trapped, the darkness perfect except for the dying violet flame and the panic in their throats.



Baz heard Lucien curse, and behind that, the growl that meant Caro had dropped the pretense and let the familiar out. Her hands went wide, fingers clawed, and she planted them on the sides of the collapse, pushing up with a sound that was not quite human.



Baz watched, spellbound, as Caro’s shoulders and spine surged, muscles roiling under skin that flickered between human and something wilder. She bared her teeth and heaved. The stone moved—just enough. Baz lunged, grabbed Lucien by the elbow, and yanked him forward. Seraphine slipped past as well, her eyes wide, pupils gone full gold.



Caro held the tunnel up for a heartbeat, two, then dove through herself as the whole mess crashed in a plume of powder and darkness. She hit the ground, rolled, and came up on all fours, panting. A fresh line of blood traced her back, dark and—under the torch—almost purple.



Baz said nothing. He pulled his jacket off—ridiculous, probably, but the adrenaline made him generous—and wrapped it around her shoulders. “You ever try being small?” he said, a little out of breath.



She glared, but didn’t shrug the jacket off. “Your concern is touching,” she said, voice sandpaper, “but your wardrobe is tragic.”



Baz snorted, then coughed, then grinned again. “Tell that to the Council next time they see me.”



Lucien took stock, checked Seraphine for injury, then looked back at Baz and Caro. “We’re almost there,” he said. “The resonance is strongest above us.”



Baz nodded, and they crawled the rest of the way in a silence that was, for once, not awkward.



The passage opened suddenly into a low chamber, round, more cave than crypt. At the center stood a rusted iron ladder, bolted into the stone and pointing straight up into whatever waited above. Baz checked the base—solid, despite the corrosion.



He turned to the group. “This puts us near Montmartre,” he said, brushing the dust from his hands. “Old rumor was it was a convent, before they turned it to wine cellars and then back to a church. Nobody but old men and pigeons up there now.”



“Far from where they’ll be watching,” Lucien finished.



Baz grinned, for once letting the exhaustion show. “It’s almost like I plan things.”



Caro was already at the ladder, her claws half out as she tested each rung. “Let’s go before I regret not dying in the tunnel.”



Baz went second, then Seraphine, then Lucien last, watching their backs as always. The climb was longer than Baz expected—forty, fifty rungs, each one biting the flesh off his hands. The torch died somewhere near the middle, but above, a new light waited—pale at first, then blazing.



At the top, Baz pushed open the hatch, and Paris night slammed him in the face. The cold was bracing, the air almost electric with magic.



He rolled onto the roof, chest heaving. The others tumbled out after, and they lay there for a moment, a heap of arms and curses and laughter.



Caro crawled to the edge, then turned over to face the sky. “Holy shit,” she whispered.



Baz joined her and saw it: The Veil above Paris, not just torn, but unraveled. An aurora raged over the rooftops, veins of violet and gold pulsing in sheets so bright they drowned out the moon. Every star burned brighter. Every shadow was alive.



Behind them, the city was quiet—but not dead. The world was holding its breath, waiting to see what happened next.



Baz stretched out, the jacket already caked with her blood and his sweat, and watched the sky change colors. For the first time in a long while, he felt something like hope—or maybe just the conviction that even the end of the world could be survived if you had the right people with you.



Lucien settled next to him, Seraphine beside that. Caro, all sharp elbows and teeth, nestled on the far side, but didn’t resist when Baz draped an arm around her shoulder.



They stayed that way for a while, wordless, as the aurora raged.



Finally, Seraphine broke the quiet: “So. The future.”



Baz rolled his head to look at her, smirk firmly in place. “Reckon it’ll need breaking in.”



Caro, voice low, purred: “Try not to fuck it up.”



Baz laughed, and it sounded like defiance, or maybe just victory.



Above, the Veil burned on, and the city learned to live in color again.

## ASSISTANT

# Chapter 17

## Scene 1

Baz’s boots hit the Paris curb like the world owed him an apology. The air was glassy with cold and the tension of a city clamped under two centuries of suppressed magic. In the half-second before the others caught up, he thumbed his lighter and kissed flame to the torch head. It flared—not orange, but a bruised violet that cut the dark and left afterimages on his vision.

Seraphine and Lucien took the steps a beat behind, breathing hard, but not from the run. That hum still rode them: the electric hunger in every surface, the pulse that kept time in their bones. Caro prowled ahead with a gait halfway between alley cat and ballet mistress—shoulders sharp, every step a quiet decision.

Baz offered a mock salute and started down the spiral. “Ladies, gentleman, and assorted felines—welcome to the ossuaries.” He kept his voice low. “Three lefts, two rights, priest’s stairwell. Watch your step. These bones don’t like being disturbed.”

The entry reeked of damp limestone and distant sewage. Baz led with the torch held high. The walls closed in with the architectural optimism of a grave. Skulls stared in recursion: neat rows, rosettes that mocked mourning, bulges of femurs and pelvises repeating like a bad prayer.

Lucien kept close, face tight, eyes flicking between the torch and Seraphine. He looked more alive than the last time Baz had seen him, which meant he looked like death in a new suit. “You know these tunnels?”

“You never forget your first revolution,” Baz said. “Or your third. All the real ones end in cellars and tunnels. Paris is a city built on old mistakes.”

Caro dropped to all fours for a stretch, tail flickering in and out of reality. At each junction she paused, ears canted, then bared her teeth and waved them on. “We’re alone for now,” she whispered, “but I smell trouble up the line.” Her eyes flashed gold, then blue. “Someone’s running a drone. Bad signature—probably a bat.”

Seraphine laughed under her breath, low and mean. “Dominion never learned subtle.”

They hit the first left. The ceiling dropped; the air went wet. The bones got older, or just more honest. Baz kept one glove on the iron rail—mining relic, more rust than metal, still the only thing that saved you from getting lost forever.

“Down here, the walls carry sound,” Baz said. “We’ll hear them before we see them.” He checked his pulse out of habit—still dead. It was always the metaphors that stung.

Seraphine moved careful. Torchlight caught her face and made her look like someone Baz could have known in another life—ferocious, tired, stubborn as rot. “If they’re after us, how long?”

Lucien answered without looking at her. “Depends whether it’s the Inquisition or a culling party.”

Caro, suddenly all human again, shivered. “Both,” she said, rubbing her forearm. “And someone’s on a resonance tracker. We’re bleeding a signal even a politician could follow.”

Baz pushed the pace. At the second right he stopped short of a pillar of skulls arranged like a wedding cake. He swept the torch. “See these eyes? Each remembers a war. If the Dominion wants to dance—let them come.”

Lucien nodded once, somewhere between camaraderie and apology. “Your guidance is appreciated.”

Baz snorted. “We’re all rats down here, Curator.”

A wider corridor yawned open: finished walls, a central path beneath an arch painted with Latin graffiti and candle smoke. The torch threw overlapping shadows—first Lucien’s, then Seraphine’s, then Caro’s slinking curse at the edge.

“Stop,” Baz said, all performance gone. “Listen.”

At first: only water and old stone. Then: a three-count of boots—steady, pause, steady—like someone had scripted the menace.

Caro’s hackles rose. She blurred toward feline. “Virelli’s men,” she hissed. “Faster than I thought.”

Something tightened under Baz’s ribs—not fear, exactly; the edge that comes before a break. “Left, left, then down. Don’t stop.”

They ran.

The tunnels narrowed. Baz clipped an elbow on a rib and kept the torch high. Caro ducked a collapsed arch and held it for the others. Stone scraped Lucien’s head, drawing a silvered bead that vanished as fast as it formed.

“You all right?” Seraphine rasped.

“Only pride,” Lucien said, hand at the line.

The descent spiraled. Condensation slicked the limestone; violet-gold veins laced the walls—faint at first, then thickening. Baz lifted the torch. Color pulsed through the seams, matching the world above.

Seraphine pressed her palm to the stone. “Resonance. Everywhere.”

Lucien’s jaw set. “Acceleration. If we don’t reach the core—”

“Survive first,” Baz cut in. “Apocalypse later.”

Caro laughed, a low growl. “He’s not wrong.”

One more left, a switchback, and they skidded onto a wide landing. Steps dove into dark. The air changed—warmth, old incense, rot.

“Priest’s stairwell,” Baz said. “Holy ground after this. Even the Dominion feels that.”

He rattled the gate for show, then picked the lock with a bar from his boot. The hinges screamed to forever.

Seraphine led down, boots whispering. Lucien followed, then Baz, Caro last—pausing to scent the tunnel.

“We’re not alone anymore,” Baz said.

Seraphine showed teeth. “We never were.”

They plunged. The resonance bled through every surface until the torch wasn’t needed; Baz lowered it, letting the magic light the steps. Above, the footfalls thickened, but speed did the work. Now and then Baz caught Caro’s pupils blown wide and knew she understood the moment: not for heroes—just survivors.

At the bottom, a corridor so tight even Lucien had to turn sideways. Ozone and memory. The walls thrummed in Baz’s teeth.

“Where?” Seraphine asked.

Baz aimed the torch at a hairline seam. “Follow the color,” he said, and for once it sounded romantic.

They did. The tunnel curved, forked, doubled back; Baz never hesitated. The pitch climbed until even Caro flinched. Lucien caught Seraphine’s arm; she didn’t shake him off.

They paused at a junction, chests heaving. Behind, boots and Latin. Ahead, the hum like a held note.

Baz set the torch on a ledge. “Point of no return,” he said. “Left, we get lost slow. Right, we hit the breach.”

“Left is dying slower,” Caro said. “Right—”

“We make history,” Baz finished.

Lucien looked to Seraphine. Her eyes burned gold and violet. “I trust you.”

“Good,” she said, sudden and savage. “Follow me.”

They went right. The tunnel narrowed, then opened into an ossuary so vast the torch couldn’t find the end. Stacks of skulls rose to the ceiling. All watching. All remembering.

Behind, boots hit the landing.

“Three lefts, two rights, priest’s stairwell, then history,” Baz muttered, scooping the torch and signaling them on. “Let’s see if Paris is ready.”

They ran. Red flared behind. Violet-gold ahead painted shadows on their backs. Panic tried for Baz’s throat; he ran harder.

The torch guttered at the next turn, but the walls glowed on their own—light from memory or hope or refusal to die quietly.

Baz barreled forward, every breath a dare. Seraphine a stride ahead, Lucien beside, Caro a flicker at his heel.

They plunged deeper, chasing the only thing that mattered: a chance to change the story before it ended.

Behind them, the world finally began to catch up.

---

## Scene 2

The next leg was a fever dream of panic and water.

They hammered down a spiral into a corridor half-choked with runoff—the kind that chews through Paris stone like nothing can stop it. First step, ice water to Baz’s calf. Second step, up to his knee. Lucien’s hissed curse warned the rest a beat too late; all of them were thigh-deep and wading through what Baz decided to call a sewer only out of optimism.

The torch sputtered. Baz shook it and cupped the flame. The violet core guttered, then surged, licking old resin on the walls so every drop glinted like mercury. For a second their reflections skinned the flood: Seraphine’s jaw set; Lucien’s face smoothed by focus; Caro half-submerged and furious at the cold.

“Well, that’s new and terrifying,” Baz said, keeping breezy by force. “If I start glowing, put me out.”

Behind, the echo of boots went liquid—slap-slap-slap—closing fast. Caro, soaked and human, still moved like joints were a rumor. She cut the water like a shark, finding every high ridge and hauling them toward it.

The tunnel dipped; water climbed. Baz kept the torch up, but the flame only just lived. Breath sawed cold.

“They’re closing,” Lucien murmured.

“I know.” Baz risked a glance back. Lamp-glow chopped and vanished around bends, but the sound promised minutes at most.

Caro paused at a cross-tunnel, sniffed, chin-pointed. “There. The vault.”

Seraphine slipped, boot skidding on limestone. She went under to the chest. Baz didn’t think; he caught her collar and yanked. She came up coughing, hair plastered, eyes bright.

“You always this charming?” she rasped.

“You haven’t seen charming,” he said, steadying her, then let go.

They pushed on. The water shallowed, then drained as the tunnel tilted. Baz counted breaths—twenty, forty—lost the math to adrenaline. The torch flared blue, smelling like burnt sugar.

Lucien’s voice close now: “We’re near. The wall hums with it.”

It did. Every few meters the limestone vibrated under Baz’s free hand, a plucked wire that ran up his arm and set his teeth buzzing.

Caro froze, arm shot out. “Wait. Trap. Not ours.”

They reached a junction: corridor wide as a nave, skulls stacked three meters high, a patch of temptingly dry stone at center.

Baz lowered the torch. The boots had gone quiet, but breathing threaded the dark. More than four.

Caro flashed a hand sign Baz hadn’t seen since barricade days—Hide. She dragged Lucien left. Baz snagged Seraphine and muscled them behind a pillar of bone. He killed the torch. Only the sickly violet bled through seams in stone.

Crouch. Press. Wait.

Footsteps returned—softer now, practiced stealth. Two? Three? Baz strained. Three. Military timing.

“They’re here,” a Dominion dog muttered.

A second voice—low, dangerous—said, “Fan out. The cat doubles back.”

Seraphine’s pulse jumped under Baz’s palm. He tightened his hand over her mouth and felt her glare burn through it.

Lights swept. The first man passed, lamp high. The second trailed with a gun and old-war patience. The third moved like a predator—Virelli’s kind. He knelt near where Caro and Lucien hid and pressed a palm to the stones.

Baz held his breath. So did Seraphine.

The man sniffed, spoke something sharp in a language Baz didn’t know. For a heartbeat Baz thought they were finished.

Caro’s voice—farther down the corridor—rang out in a perfect, echoing meow. Bold as a shot.

All three hunters pivoted and sprinted toward the sound.

Baz lifted his hand. Seraphine exhaled murder and gratitude. “Next time,” she whispered, “I bite.”

He winked. “Promises, promises.”

They waited until the voices thinned. Caro and Lucien reappeared—dripping, grinning like thieves.

“Nice play,” Baz said.

Caro flicked water. “Dominion men. Brains like cinder blocks. Show them a shiny—”

“They chase,” Lucien finished, straightening his cuffs. “We keep moving. The breach isn’t far.”

“Which way?” Seraphine asked.

Baz relit the torch. It sputtered, then steadied. “This way,” he said, and heard the certainty in his own voice—as if the tunnel wasn’t a tomb but a road, and every wrong turn could still end somewhere worth it.

They slipped into the vault. The skulls watched. For the first time Baz felt almost at home.

Behind, the water stilled. The hunters faded, for now.

Ahead, the walls began to breathe with the color of another world.

Baz led. If his hand shook on the torch, it was only because there was so much left to burn.

---

## Scene 3

The tunnel gave them one last indignity before it let them go.

They spilled into a cul-de-sac of bone and mortar, torchlight dying on stone, all four of them mud-streaked and gulping air. Baz recovered first—he always did—and pressed a palm to the wall, feeling for the trick his grandfather swore by.

Caro stood very still, eyes closed, catching every flicker of sound. The world might have ended, and Baz wouldn’t have noticed; he was counting under his breath. When the rhythm clicked he glanced at Caro. She nodded once, sharp.

No words needed.

He set his shoulder to a panel and shoved. Stone grated. Dust coughed. The wall gave—just enough. A coffin’s width of dark opened.

“Revolution leaves marks,” he said, and couldn’t keep the pride out of his voice even through the ache.

Lucien’s brow rose, approving. “I’d heard the cells kept their doors.”

“Detours,” Baz corrected, grinning.

He ducked in, torch first. The passage was tight and lower still. Within three steps they had to hunch; within ten, crawl. Old air bit the nose with ammonia and centuries. Above, Paris and bone groaned.

Baz led on hands and knees, torch in his teeth, refusing to look back. Seraphine came after, then Lucien, then Caro—barely fitting even thin.

The sound changed first—a tremor in the dust, a shudder that ran up Baz’s hands into the marrow. The rumble followed, soft and ominous, like a cough from the world’s old lungs.

“Move!” Baz barked. “We’re about to—”

The ceiling sagged. A slab dropped behind Lucien, blocking the path. For a beat they were trapped—perfect dark save for the dying violet flame and the panic in their throats.

Lucien cursed. Caro stopped pretending to be merely human. She planted clawed hands on the sagging stone and pushed with a sound that wasn’t meant for churches.

Muscle rippled; skin flickered. Stone shifted. Baz lunged, hooked Lucien’s elbow, yanked. Seraphine slipped through, eyes full gold.

Caro held the tunnel a heartbeat more, then dove as the roof collapsed in a plume of powder. She rolled and came up on all fours, panting. A fresh line of blood traced her back, slick and—under the torch—almost purple.

Baz stripped off his jacket and wrapped it over her shoulders without ceremony. “You ever try being small?”

She glared but didn’t shrug him off. “Your concern is touching,” she rasped. “Your wardrobe is tragic.”

“Tell the Council that,” he coughed, grinning.

Lucien scanned for injuries, checked Seraphine, then looked to Baz and Caro. “Almost there. Strongest above.”

They crawled the last stretch in a silence that, for once, didn’t chafe.

The passage opened into a round chamber—more cave than crypt. A rusted iron ladder waited at center, bolted into stone and pointing straight up.

“Montmartre,” Baz said, brushing dust off. “Was a convent. Then wine cellars. Now a church. Pigeons and old men.”

“Far from the Dominion’s eye,” Lucien said.

“Almost like I plan things,” Baz muttered.

Caro tested the rungs with half-out claws. “Up, before I regret not dying heroic.”

Baz went second, Seraphine after, Lucien last—watching backs as always. The climb was longer than it looked: forty, fifty rungs, each one biting skin. The torch died halfway, but a new light bled down—pale, then blazing.

Baz shoved the hatch and Paris night slammed his face. Cold and electric. The air itself felt charged.

He rolled onto the roof and gulped sky. The others tumbled out. For a moment they lay tangled—arms, curses, laughter.

Caro crawled to the edge and stared up. “Holy shit,” she breathed.

Baz joined her. The Veil above Paris wasn’t torn; it was unraveled. An aurora raged, violet and gold veining the night in sheets so bright they drowned the moon. Stars burned harder. Shadows breathed.

Behind them the city held its breath. Not dead—waiting.

Baz stretched out, jacket already caked with her blood and his sweat, and let the sky repaint him. For the first time in a long time he felt something like hope—or the conviction that even endings can be survived if the right people are beside you.

Lucien settled next to him. Seraphine beside him. Caro—all elbows and teeth—on the far side, not resisting when Baz slung an arm over her shoulders.

They stayed like that, wordless, while the aurora raged.

Finally Seraphine said, “So. The future.”

Baz rolled his head to meet her eyes, smirk returning. “Reckon it’ll need breaking in.”

Caro purred, low. “Try not to fuck it up.”

Baz laughed, the sound halfway between defiance and victory.

Above, the Veil burned on, and the city learned, slowly, to live in color again.

## USER

Repeat for Chapter 18: 

# Scene 1



They ran on memory. Every footfall counted: down the spiral, into wet black, with nothing behind them but the crash of boots and the promise of old bones. Baz led, torch gone, navigating by the stink of centuries and the hand-rattling shiver of limestone veins. Every few yards he spat out a curse or a half-invented map. It kept them moving and stopped the dark from swallowing what was left of their courage.



Lucien’s watch threw a weak, phosphor pulse—a cold tick of light, its glow skipping half-beats as if even the mechanism doubted survival. Seraphine’s amulet bled gold to nothing, then rallied, then nearly guttered out. The deeper they plunged, the worse it got. Above, the Veil’s light might as well have been a rumor.



Caro called the turns: “Left. Keep left. Dead end ahead, don’t trust the ladder.” Her voice slurred, not quite human, but sharp enough to wake adrenaline in every nerve. Once, she flickered from girl to cat and back midstride, body not sure what form to fear in. The echo bounced through the low arches, doubled, then came back at them in a foreign cadence.



The catacombs stank of wet rot and calcium. The air thickened with each switchback. At the third junction, Lucien hesitated, fingers tight on the cold lip of a broken font, scanning for any sign of another route. The stone vibrated under his palm—a living pulse, distant but rising.



“Baz—” Lucien started.



“Yeah, I know.” Baz’s voice was low and mean, but he didn’t look back. “Pick your poison: parish ossuary, or we try the old Napoleonic drainage.” He swung them left without waiting for consensus.



Seraphine slipped. The mud at her heel went out from under her, and she slammed shoulder-first into a wall lined with femurs. The shock reset her vision: one split second of the present, then a dozen flickers of the future—water, blackness, teeth, then a shimmer she couldn’t parse.



Caro steadied her, hands icy. “Up. Don



Caro steadied her, hands icy. “Up. Don’t stall now.” She braced a knee against the slick ossuary wall and leveraged Seraphine to standing. The echo behind them thickened—boots and voices, the Dominion’s certainty coming on hard.



Baz checked the next junction. He ducked the lintel, cursed, and then flattened back to the others. “Blockage. New.” He spat wet dust, looked at Lucien. “That on your map, genius?”



“It’s a service tunnel,” Lucien answered, all dry, tight focus. “It shouldn’t—” He pressed lips thin, recalibrated. “Go right, cut left, follow the old waterline.”



They ran. The tunnel funneled into a bend so sharp Baz nearly missed it. Caro dropped cat, sprinted low to the floor. Lucien and Seraphine followed, pressed close. Seraphine clutched her chest where the amulet gnawed at bone.



Baz’s coat snagged on wire. He tore free, not caring if it ripped. “Past here,” he gasped, “we’re under the eighteenth. Used to be a cache.”



Seraphine’s lungs ripped at each breath. “You sure?”



He managed a grin that was all bone. “Not remotely.” He gave her a hand anyway, pulled her into the dark.



The air changed. The humidity jumped; the floor sloped, water pooling along both sides. Above, the ceiling dropped, cracked in two places by old Paris settling on itself.



Caro led now, nose to the water. Her voice came back: “Careful. Air’s different. Smells—” She hesitated. “Burned ozone.”



Lucien dropped a hand to the wall, using the vibration as a sensor. “We’re close to the surface. If we find a breach, we can—” His words stopped. From ahead: the clatter of rocks, a new voice, and then a complete, wet silence.



Baz and Seraphine reached Caro at the same instant. She’d frozen, tail puffed and hands halfway claws. Ahead, the tunnel collapsed. A solid wall of stone, brick, femur. The caved-in roof still shed pebbles. Dust shimmered gold and silver in the low watchlight.



Baz moved first, hands raking for leverage. The stone bled years of slime, but he was stronger than he looked. “Help me,” he hissed.



Seraphine leaned in, hands on damp bone, pushing with everything left. “Caro, wedge the right.” Caro morphed to cat, vanished into a tiny fracture, reappeared a second later. “No way through. Solid for meters.” Her voice vibrated with animal panic.



Behind them, the tunnel’s echo tripled: voices, then the harsh flick of flashlights on stone.



“We’re cornered,” Baz said, sweat streaking his face. “They’ll bottleneck us and gas the hole.”



Lucien ran his fingers along the stone. “There’s—wait.” He tapped a rhythm, then listened. “A pocket above. Pressure is off-balance. If we can—” He looked at Seraphine. “Can you force it? With the amulet?”



Seraphine wanted to laugh. “It’s running on fumes,” she said, but held the copper in her palm. She closed her eyes, searched for the gold core inside, and met only the dregs of a field once unbreakable. Still, she flexed. The brand at her collarbone flared, then dulled to nothing.



“Keep pushing,” Lucien said, voice nearly breaking. “It doesn’t have to last—just crack the top.”



Caro braced herself and dug claws in for grip. Baz grabbed Lucien’s arm, interlaced fingers for a human wedge. Together, they drove at the wall.



The debris above them gave a low, aching groan. A web of fractures shimmered through the block, but nothing gave.



Behind: a shout. Baz’s blood went cold. “They’re close. Two minutes, maybe less.”



Seraphine clung to the stone, ignoring the pain in her hands, and tried again. This time she forced the memory: the first breach, the time she lit half the parish in gold. She called up the burn, focused it to a point, and imagined the cave-in as just another door to open.



A tremor ran through the wall. A wet gasp of air, then a trickle of light—thin and violet, like dawn after a funeral.



For one suspended moment, everything stilled.



The light didn’t belong to Lucien’s watch or Seraphine’s amulet. It glowed through the rubble, steady, impossibly clean. For the length of a heartbeat, every one of them felt it: not just a presence, but an invitation.



Baz blinked sweat from his eyes. “You all see that?”



“Not a torch,” Lucien said, voice dry and paper-thin. “Not anything I’ve catalogued.”



Caro went human, hair haloed by dust. “There’s a pocket,” she whispered. “And someone’s in it.”



Seraphine’s arms shook, but she held the line. The gold of her bruise thinned and mingled with the violet.



From the other side of the collapse, a figure resolved: thin, adolescent, wearing old city clothes. She looked sixteen, maybe, but her eyes glowed out of time. The girl raised one hand, palm out. The light thickened, then suffused the stone until it was more shadow than matter.



Lucien stared, every calculation gone. “This is impossible,” he said, voice lost between hope and terror.



Baz didn’t blink. “That’s what they said about me.”



The Dominion’s boots thudded, echo growing. “We’re out of time,” Baz hissed. “If she can open it—”



The girl moved closer. The wall between them evaporated to a membrane of violet haze. Up close, her face was not right: features blurring at the edges, an overlay of child and ancient. Her smile was a sad crack. She mouthed a word none of them could hear, but Seraphine felt it in her gut.



“Hybrid,” Caro whispered, as if naming it made it real.



The girl reached for Seraphine’s hand through the debris.



Seraphine, shaking, reached back.



The instant they touched, the resonance surged, the gold and violet interlaced, and the cave-in dissolved. The air cleared, cold and electric. The path lay open, though nothing should have moved.



The girl beckoned them forward.



Baz didn’t wait for debate. He dragged Lucien by the shoulder, Caro shoved at Seraphine’s back, and they ran through the breach and into the impossible, the violet light guiding them deeper into the bone of Paris.



They never looked back to see if the Dominion dared follow.





# Scene 2



The violet-lit girl took two steps forward and the dust bent away from her, as if even air respected the claim she made on the space. Her sneakers hit bone and left no mark; her hands, when she brushed them over the ruined wall, sent fractures in every direction—at once spiderweb and stitch. Her face, caught between childhood and the sharpness of the very old, shimmered at the edges, a strobe of every girl who’d ever died in Paris and every one who might yet live.



Baz was the first to risk speech. “Well, that’s convenient,” he said, more for the benefit of fear than for comedy. He kept a respectful distance, torch now just a club.



Lucien, always the scientist, measured the girl with a gaze that edged on worship. His hand hovered over the watch; its tick now perfect, each pulse in time with the violet heartbeat radiating from the newcomer. He cleared his throat but found no words.



Seraphine’s chest ached. She pressed two fingers to the brand at her collarbone, found it—impossibly—alive with the same spectral color. Each throb under her skin matched the rhythm in the stone. The copper amulet, still warm from the last push, lit up in answer, but the gold had been overtaken by a sheen of cold indigo. The sensation wasn’t pain, but something like longing.



Caro was a silhouette in the shifting light—cat tail, human hair, back again in the span of a second. Her fur stood high as she prowled the periphery, every step reluctant. “Not a witch,” she muttered, just audible to Seraphine. “Not a vampire. Not even a ghost.” She watched the girl with the fixed stare of a predator considering a thunderstorm.



The girl ignored them all. She reached for the collapse, palms up. With a tiny, deliberate movement, she pressed her right index finger to the largest boulder and swept it through the air as if writing a line on water. Violet current bled from her skin into the stone, which shivered and bled cracks that instantly sealed themselves in a lattice of impossible geometry.



Where there had been impassable ruin, a narrow corridor took shape. The ceiling and floor fused with veins of purple fire, giving just enough room for a body, two if they ducked. The air smelled briefly of lavender and scorched ozone.



“Brava,” Baz breathed.



The girl pivoted toward them. Her smile was wrong—too practiced for her age, or maybe too raw. She said nothing, only beckoned with a finger and started down the new passage, her violet glow already lighting the next meter ahead.



Seraphine went first, because she had to. The stone under her hand felt new, almost living, and the corridor’s curve seemed to guide rather than confine. Lucien came next, then Caro, then Baz taking up the rear with the remains of the torch.



The girl kept a polite distance, never turning to check if they followed, but always close enough that her presence overwrote the chill and the dark.



After the first twenty yards, Seraphine risked it. “Who are you?” she asked, voice stripped of challenge.



The girl paused. She looked at Seraphine, straight through the surface, then at Lucien, then at the others. When she finally spoke, the words were soft and round, but carried in the stone and the marrow.



“I’m not supposed to be here,” she said, accentless, the edges of her mouth barely lifting. “But neither are you.”



Caro twitched, half-laughed, half-snapped. “Are you going to help us, or just lead us deeper?”



The girl didn’t answer, but as she moved forward, she left a ghost-handprint of light on the wall. It faded only slowly, as if time itself needed a minute to adjust.



Baz shrugged. “I’ve followed worse.”



Lucien’s hand didn’t leave the watch. “This isn’t a rescue,” he said, half to himself. “It’s a message.”



Seraphine felt the resonance at her collarbone intensify until her heart had no choice but to follow. She reached out, let her hand hover over the girl’s last print. It was warm. It hummed.



They walked on, guided by the pulse and the light, into a future they could barely imagine.





# Scene 3



The corridor bent around logic. Walls narrowed, then widened; the floor pitched up, then dropped again, always just slightly wrong. The further they went, the more Seraphine felt her own body vibrate in sympathy to the girl’s light. Each handprint the girl left on the wall seemed to glow longer, and Seraphine realized they weren’t fading at all but stacking, layer upon layer, in a trail that would outlive any of them.



Baz lagged once to check a side path. When he looked back, the tunnel behind was sealed by a slab of black glass, cold and perfect, cutting off even memory of pursuit.



“We’re further down than the Metro,” Lucien said, scanning the mineral veins overhead. “We should be hitting groundwater. Or magma.” His voice thinned as he tried to calculate. “But I don’t—”



“Don’t,” Caro muttered, ears flattening as she flicked between forms. “The geometry’s hungry. Best not to feed it.”



They kept pace with the girl, who moved without haste, every step deliberate. Once, at a collapsed arch, she stopped and ran a thumb along the fracture, leaving it soldered and whole in her wake. Lucien watched with the greedy eyes of a child, but when he tried to touch the mend, it repelled him with a low harmonic shock.



Seraphine risked a question, “Are you alive?” but the girl ignored her, though Seraphine’s own mark stung in sympathetic answer.



Another ten paces, the tunnel opened. They entered a chamber so wide and old it felt like standing inside a cathedral’s nave, built from nothing but human bone and melted calcium. Five tunnels met here, each mouth ringed by a mandible arch. The girl stopped at the center, turned, and finally looked at each of them as if weighing their presence in the scales of a very personal justice.



Her face stilled for the first time, nothing flickering, no age but the present. Her eyes matched the resonance in the wall—pure, clean, dangerous.



Seraphine felt the hush coil. Even Baz seemed scared to speak.



The girl spoke, voice a smooth, cold ripple: “The Veil thins where it should be strongest.” She held the silence, made them swallow it.



Then, with a single, sure gesture, she pointed to the second tunnel from the left—darker than the rest, but glimmering faint at the edges.



“Thank you?” Baz offered, all nerves and old manners.



The girl nodded, almost sad, and turned. As she walked, her outline fuzzed, details smearing until she was a silhouette, then a skeleton of light, then a drift of dust that caught in the new tunnel’s wind and vanished.



Caro followed, nose down, pawing at the spot where the girl had been. “She’s gone,” Caro said, but her tone was all reverence. “Or everywhere.”



Lucien’s watch ticked, then stilled, then ticked again, now perfectly in time with the resonance lining the tunnel.



Baz touched the wall, half-expecting it to bite. “I’ve had worse tour guides,” he said, quietly, “but none who glowed.”



Seraphine looked at her chest. The brand was healed, brighter than ever, a new pattern etched at its edge: five points, connected by a circle. The amulet at her throat shone in the dim, pulse matching the mark. She smiled, then shook.



Caro flicked to human, looked at Seraphine’s collar. “You always get the best party favors.”



“Occupational hazard,” Seraphine said, not sure if she wanted to laugh or cry.



Baz pointed with his chin. “Should we—?”



“We follow,” Lucien said, already moving. He paused just long enough to say, very softly, “I believe we’ve just met our first hybrid.”



Caro let the echo carry. It didn’t fade, just settled in as part of the air.



Together they crossed the nave of bone and turned down the tunnel the girl had chosen for them, the world behind them already closing to legend.

## Scene 1

They ran on memory. Every footfall counted—down the spiral, into wet black—with nothing behind them but the crash of boots and the promise of old bones. Baz led, torch gone, navigating by the stink of centuries and the hand-rattling shiver of limestone veins. Every few yards he spat a curse or half-invented a map. It kept them moving and kept the dark from swallowing what was left of their courage.

Lucien’s watch threw a weak, phosphor pulse—a cold tick of light, its glow skipping half-beats as if even the mechanism doubted survival. Seraphine’s amulet bled gold to nothing, rallied, then guttered again. The deeper they plunged, the worse it got. Above, the Veil’s light might as well have been a rumor.

Caro called the turns. “Left. Keep left. Dead end ahead—don’t trust the ladder.” Her voice slurred at the edges—not quite human—but bright enough to wake every nerve. Once she flickered from girl to cat and back midstride, as if her body couldn’t decide what shape fear wore best. The echo took her orders, doubled them, and threw them back in a foreign cadence.

The catacombs stank of wet rot and calcium. The air thickened with each switchback. At the third junction, Lucien hesitated, fingers white on the cold lip of a broken font, scanning for any sign of another route. The stone vibrated under his palm—a living pulse, distant but rising.

“Baz—” Lucien started.

“Yeah, I know.” Baz’s voice was low and mean, but he didn’t look back. “Pick your poison: parish ossuary or the old Napoleonic drainage.” He swung them left without waiting for consensus.

Seraphine slipped. Mud went out from under her heel, and she slammed shoulder-first into a wall of femurs. The jolt reset her vision: a split second of now, then a dozen flickers of later—water, blackness, teeth, and a shimmer she couldn’t parse.

Caro steadied her, hands icy. “Up. Don’t stall now.” She braced a knee against the slick ossuary wall and levered Seraphine to standing. The echo behind them thickened—boots and voices, the Dominion’s certainty coming on hard.

Baz checked the next junction, ducked the lintel, cursed, and flattened back to them. “Blockage. New.” He spat wet dust and cut a look at Lucien. “That on your map, genius?”

“Service tunnel,” Lucien said, all dry, tight focus. “It shouldn’t—” He pressed his lips thin, recalibrating. “Go right, cut left, follow the old waterline.”

They ran. The tunnel funneled into a bend so sharp Baz nearly missed it. Caro dropped cat, sprinted low. Lucien and Seraphine pressed close. Seraphine clutched her chest where the amulet gnawed at bone.

Baz’s coat snagged on wire. He tore free, not caring if it ripped. “Past here,” he gasped, “we’re under the Eighteenth. Used to be a cache.”

Seraphine’s lungs scraped like glass. “You sure?”

“Not remotely.” He gave her a hand anyway and pulled her into the dark.

The air changed. Humidity jumped. The floor sloped; water pooled on both sides. Overhead the ceiling dropped, cracked in two places by old Paris settling on itself.

Caro led now, nose to the water. “Careful,” came her voice. “Air’s different. Smells—” She hesitated. “Burned ozone.”

Lucien dropped a hand to the wall, reading vibration. “Close to the surface. If we find a breach, we can—” He stopped. From ahead: the clatter of rock, a stranger’s breath, then a complete, wet silence.

Caro morphed to cat and slid into a narrow fracture, then reappeared a beat later. “No way through. Solid for meters.” Her voice vibrated with panic older than language.

Behind them, the tunnel’s echo tripled: voices, the hard flick of flashlights on stone.

“We’re cornered,” Baz said, sweat cutting tracks through dust. “They’ll bottleneck us and gas the hole.”

Lucien ran his fingers along the wall, counting in the tremor. “There’s—wait.” He tapped a rhythm, listened. “A pocket above. Pressure’s off-balance. If we can—” He looked at Seraphine. “Can you force it? With the amulet?”

Seraphine wanted to laugh. “It’s running on fumes,” she said, but cradled the copper in her palm. She closed her eyes, hunted for the gold core, and met only dregs. Still, she flexed. The brand at her collarbone flared—then dulled.

“Keep pushing,” Lucien said, voice near breaking. “It doesn’t have to last. Just crack the top.”

Caro dug claws in for grip. Baz locked hands with Lucien to make a human wedge. Together, they drove at the wall.

The debris gave a low, aching groan. A web of fractures shimmered through the block, but nothing let go.

Behind: a shout, closer now. Baz’s blood went cold. “Two minutes. Maybe less.”

Seraphine clung to the stone, ignoring the pain in her hands, and tried again. She forced memory—the first breach, the parish lit gold. She brought the burn forward, focused it to a point, and imagined the cave-in as just another door.

A tremor ran through the wall. A wet gasp of air. Then a trickle of light—thin and violet, like dawn after a funeral.

For one suspended moment, everything stilled.

The light wasn’t Lucien’s watch or Seraphine’s amulet. It glowed through the rubble—steady, impossibly clean. For the length of a heartbeat each of them felt it: not just a presence, but an invitation.

“You see that?” Baz panted.

“Not a torch,” Lucien said, voice paper-thin. “Not anything I’ve catalogued.”

Caro went human, dust haloing her hair. “There’s a pocket,” she whispered. “And someone’s in it.”

Seraphine’s arms shook, but she held the line. The gold of her bruise thinned, mingled with violet.

From the other side, a figure resolved—thin, adolescent, in old city clothes. Sixteen, maybe. Her eyes glowed out of time. She raised one hand, palm out. The light thickened, suffusing stone until it was more shadow than matter.

Lucien stared, all calculations burned away. “Impossible,” he said, voice caught between hope and terror.

“That’s what they said about me,” Baz muttered, not blinking.

Boots pounded closer. “If she can open it—” Baz growled.

The girl moved nearer. The wall evaporated to a membrane of violet haze. Up close her face wasn’t right—edges blurring, an overlay of child and ancient. Her smile was a sad crack. She mouthed a word none of them heard, but Seraphine felt it in her gut.

“Hybrid,” Caro breathed, as if naming it made it take shape.

The girl reached through what remained of the collapse. Seraphine, shaking, reached back.

The instant their fingers touched, resonance surged. Gold and violet interlaced, and the cave-in dissolved. The air cleared—cold, electric. The path lay open though nothing should have moved.

The girl beckoned.

Baz didn’t wait for debate. He dragged Lucien by the shoulder. Caro shoved at Seraphine’s back. They ran through the breach and into the impossible, the violet guiding them deeper into the bone of Paris.

None of them looked back to see if the Dominion dared follow.

## Scene 3

The corridor bent around logic. Walls narrowed, then breathed wide; the floor pitched up, then dropped—always a few degrees wrong. Ten more paces and the tunnel opened. A chamber waited—wide and old as a cathedral’s nave, built of human bone and melted calcium. Five tunnels met here, each mouth ringed by a mandible arch. The girl stopped at the center, turned, and weighed them one by one, as if judging in a court with only personal laws.

Her face stilled—no flicker, no age but now. Her eyes matched the wall’s resonance: pure, clean, dangerous.

Silence coiled. Even Baz seemed scared to break it.

“Thank you?” Baz offered, nerves dressed in old manners.

She nodded—almost sad—and turned. As she walked, her outline fuzzed, smeared, thinned: first a silhouette, then a skeleton of light, then a drift of dust that rode a new wind down the chosen tunnel and vanished.

Lucien’s watch ticked, stilled, ticked again—now perfectly in time with the tunnel’s hum.

Seraphine looked down. The brand on her chest had healed brighter than ever, a new pattern etched at its edge: five points, linked by a circle. The amulet pulsed to match. She smiled, then shook.

“Occupational hazard,” Seraphine said, unsure if she wanted to laugh or cry.

Baz tilted his chin at the dark mouth. “So—?”

“We follow,” Lucien said, already moving. He paused just long enough to add, very softly, “I believe we’ve met our first hybrid.”

Caro let the word echo. It didn’t fade. It simply joined the air.
